**@SMFRA** (👍0 · 2023-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hKloP6atKXs&lc=UgxYJYtmheUDNB4qVtl4AaABAg
  Hi, Adam. I hope this humble comment finds you well. I'm sorry to say this but I think you're mistaken when you said that there is no difference in meaning when it comes to the polarity of the tag question. In the first example you use, that's (You like pizza, don't you? / You don't like pizza, do you?). In fact, the meaning and interpretation of both questions depend on the context. The first sentence with the negative tag question means that the one asking the question is surprised because the other person seems to have liked pizza before but he/she has refused to eat pizza at the time. While in the second sentence with the positive tag question, it seems that the other person does not usually like pizza but, for some reason, he/she suddenly likes it and wants to eat. So, the whole thing depends on the context. I hope you got my point and elaborate on this explanation with your opinion. Thanks in advance.

**@slaozel1972** (👍0 · 2024-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ls7-y_r6bHk&lc=UgxjWVYV9T73PQ7mh4d4AaABAg
  i'm writing below to learn all expressions meanings in video:)
  1. couldn't care less: really don't care
  2. regardless: whatever happened
  3. for all intents and purposes: almost completely done
  4. self-deprecating: putting ourselves down / lowering my own value
  5. ex-patriate: remove someone from a country
  6. nip it in the bud: nip=cut *before sth grows too big to control, you should cut and you want nip it in the bud
  7. peace of mind: everything is okey, relax, no worries, no doubts
  8. piece of my mind: i will tell you exactly what i think
  9. sleight of hand: trick, you don't actually know what happened
  10. 1st come, 1st serveD 
  11.whet your appetite: whet meanings to sharpen like knife *you make it sharper or stronger to help you enjoy the meal
  12. deep-seated: sth to sitted deep inside of you and so hard to get out or change
  13. hunger pangs
  14. case in point: sth that you use as an example to support your idea
  
  if i did any mistakes please correct me:)

  Wow, I suddenly feel much better about my English. My primary sources of education in the language for the first decade was elementary and middle school (started learning at age 7, learned very little), then, for roughly another decade, video games with a dictionary, and in the last decade - first programming documentation and the Internet, then I gradually started watching movies and TV shows, but have only read less than a dozen books total. And I still knew the correct variants of most of the expressions, especially "self-deprecating". Took me measly 30 years to reach a decent proficiency level 😁 Jokes aside, I suspect it is much easier to learn English living in an English-speaking country, as opposed to Russia. And it is definitely easier, at least to me, to learn languages with a fully developed brain (that's a bold statement, isn't it).

  I am bilingual. Been bilingual my whole life, I even dream in 2 languages. However my children speak English as their first language. And like you, they too don't have an accent. Nobody can pinpoint their ethncity or nationality by just listening to them. The reason being I am bilingual, my wife is not, English was never her spoken language until later in life. And we are from different ethnic groups. And I guess as a result neither me nor my wife's English 'have influenced our children's. Apart from us, school teachers and English-speaking relatives, American television was another primary source of listening for my kids.
       When they are in school in an English-speaking country now, they have to attend certain extra lessons for non-native speakers, but yet they score on top of the class in English language exams, surpassing their native-speaker classmates. In fact my youngest daughter was the top performer in an English language exam.
       They find it weird having to be referred to as non-native speakers by the school. But after listening to you, I understand why they aren't native speakers. They are not native Scottish English speakers.  Thanks. I guess they'll never be native speakers of any language.

  I was in the same situation before. I was at the office at that time, and I didn't know what to respond when one of the native speakers said, "What's news?". It was literally the first time for me hear such question, like what news was she talking about? Later I found out that "What's news" actually means "any recent things you want to tell me? Or something like that. For me, it was embarrassing because English is not my first language and I felt like a fool at that time. I don't know if it was just me, but I felt like I was being judged after I failed to respond the question because everything just turned into a completely awkward silence. 
  
  After hearing your story where you didn't know what your British and Australian friends were talking about, I came to realize that even native speakers have some unknown words to them, especially if they have never heard those words before. 
  
  Now a quick question, Mr. Adam. How would you respond to your students if one of them said that your English is not good just because you don't know some slangs or words that they asked you?
